Wait, let me re-read: "Before the switch time, score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**. From the switch time onwards, score increases by 1 for every robot that is **moving**."

So:
- Before switch: penalizes stationary robots → robots should **MOVE**
- After switch: penalizes moving robots → robots should **STOP**
- The green wall lights up AT the switch time as a signal

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the results:**
The champion (score 7252.44) is a simple 2-state FSM:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan=6) — moves away from walls, keeping robots moving
- Transition to state 1 when it sees green (color 2) with prob 0.9, OR with tiny prob 0.003
- State 1: Stop — stays still
- Transition back to state 0 with tiny prob 0.005

This works beautifully: robots move (away from walls) before the switch, then when the green wall lights up, they see it and stop.

**Key insight:** The green wall is color 2. The robots need to:
1. Move before switch time (to avoid penalty for being stationary)
2. Stop after switch time (to avoid penalty for moving)
3. Use the green wall (color 2) as the signal to stop

**Strategy for exploitation:**
- Tune the probabilities on the champion (p0x0, p0x1, p1x0)
- Try different movement behaviors before the green signal
- Try slightly different transition probabilities

**Strategy for exploration:**
- Try GoToColor(green) as intermediate state
- Try attraction/repulsion behaviors before stopping
- Try 3-state machines with more robust stopping

Let me be careful about the format errors from last round. Key rules:
- Every transition needs `--n<I>x<K> D` (destination), `--c<I>x<K> C` (condition), and condition params
- Condition C=3,4 need integer `--p` and real `--w`
- Condition C=7 needs `--l` and `--p`

**14 machines:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Exploit: higher green detection prob (0.95)
3. Exploit: lower return prob from stop (0.001)
4. Exploit: exploration instead of GoAway
5. Exploit: GoAway with green detection + neighbor condition
6. Exploit: Repulsion before stop
7. Exploit: Attraction before stop on green
8. Explore: 3-state Explore→GoToGreen→Stop
9. Explore: 2-state with GoToColor(green) as mover
10. Explore: 3-state GoAway→Stop with neighbor backup
11. Exploit: champion with different movement (rwm)
12. Exploit: higher p for green detection
13. Explore: 3-state repulsion→goaway→stop
14. Explore: 2-state stop-first then move

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**

The champion (6055.22) is a clean 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan=6) — robots move away from walls, staying mobile
- Transition to Stop when green (color 2) is seen with p=0.99, OR with tiny random prob 0.001
- State 1: Stop — completely still
- Transition back to State 0 with tiny prob 0.001 (almost never returns)

The key parameters that matter:
- p0x0 = 0.99 (very high prob to stop when green seen) — higher is better
- p0x1 = 0.001 (very low random transition) — lower is better  
- p1x0 = 0.001 (very low prob to restart moving) — lower is better

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try p0x0 = 1.0 (but that might not be valid, keep 0.999)
- Try even lower p0x1 and p1x0 (0.0005, 0.0001)
- Try adding a second green-detection condition from stop state (to prevent re-movement)
- Try the 3-state variant that also performed well (6980)

**Exploration strategy:**
- Try GoAwayColor with different colors as movement
- Try adding neighbor-based transitions
- Try 3-state with intermediate GoToColor(green)
- Try with p0x0 very close to 1.0

The core insight: moving away from walls (cyan=6) keeps robots moving, and detecting green (=2) triggers stopping. Very high detection probability and very low "escape" probability is optimal.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis:**

The champion (5609.00) and top performers are converging on:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan=6), p0x0=0.999 for green detection, p0x1=0.001 random
- State 1: Stop, p1x0=0.0005 (very low escape probability)

Key observations:
1. p0x0 = 0.999 (detecting green, color 2) is optimal — very high
2. p0x1 = 0.001 random fallback is good, but 0.0005 may be better
3. p1x0 = 0.0005 is better than 0.001 (stay stopped longer)
4. The 3-state variant with GoToColor(green) intermediate also performs well (~5759)
5. Multiple green detections in state 0 (transitions 3) also works well (~5752)

**Exploitation:**
- Try p0x0 = 1.0 (maximum detection)
- Try p0x1 even lower (0.0001)
- Try p1x0 even lower (0.0001)
- Try the dual green detection with even lower fallback probs
- Tweak the 3-state GoToGreen variant

**Exploration:**
- Try GoAwayColor with color 1 (red) instead of 6 (cyan)
- Try adding neighbor condition as backup trigger
- Try 3-state with attraction intermediate
- Try different color combinations

The pattern is clear: move fast away from walls (cyan), stop instantly on green, almost never restart. Let me push the parameters further toward the optimal.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of convergence:**

The champion (5448.78) uses:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan=6), p0x0=0.999 (green detection), p0x1=0.001 (random fallback)
- State 1: Stop, p1x0=0.0005 (very low escape)

Interestingly, the scores 5714.54 appear multiple times for different machines - this suggests those machines are effectively identical in behavior (same score = same stochastic outcome). The key variants:
- p0x1=0.001 vs 0.0005: champion uses 0.001, but 0.0005 also appears in 2nd/3rd
- p1x0=0.0005 (champion) vs 0.0001 (others at 5714)

The champion has p0x1=0.001 > p1x0=0.0005. The 5714 variants have p0x1=0.0005 > p1x0=0.0001.

**Key insight:** The random fallback p0x1 helps robots that can't see the green wall still eventually stop. Too low and some robots never stop. The champion's balance (0.001/0.0005) seems slightly better.

**Exploitation targets:**
- Try p0x1=0.002 (slightly higher random fallback)
- Try p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.0001
- Try p0x1=0.0005, p1x0=0.0005
- Try p0x1=0.002, p1x0=0.0005

**Avoid:** Multiple green detection transitions (3-transition variants performed worse), exploration-based movement (rwm performed poorly).

**Exploration:**
- Try GoAwayColor with color 3,4,5 (different wall colors)
- Try 3-state GoAway→Stop with neighbor condition
- Try p0x0=1.0 (but that's not a valid real... wait, 1.0 is valid)

Let me try p0x0=1.0 explicitly (if green seen, ALWAYS stop).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of convergence:**

The champion (4896.57) and its tied 3-state variant both use:
- p0x0 = 0.999 (green detection probability)
- p0x1 = 0.001 (random fallback to stop)
- p1x0 = 0.0001 (almost never restart moving)

Key findings:
- p1x0 = 0.0001 is clearly better than 0.0005 (champion vs 5230 score)
- p0x1 = 0.001 seems optimal (0.0005 gives worse scores ~5591, 6043)
- p0x0 = 0.999 vs 0.99 makes almost no difference (4896.57 vs 4896.65)
- The 3-state GoAway→GoToGreen→Stop variant ties with the 2-state

**What to try next:**
- p1x0 = 0.00005 or 0.00001 (even lower escape from stop)
- p0x1 = 0.0015 (between 0.001 and 0.002)
- p0x1 = 0.001 with different p1x0 values around 0.0001
- The 3-state variant with tweaked probabilities
- Try adding a neighbor-based condition in stop state (below threshold = stop confirmed)
- Try p0x0 = 1.0 exactly

The score 4896.57 has been achieved by two different machines, suggesting we're near a local optimum. The remaining improvement must come from even finer parameter tuning or structural changes.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of current state:**

The champion (4896.57) remains unchanged. Key observations from round 7:

1. **p1x0 = 0** (machine #2, score 5054.32) — setting escape probability to exactly 0 is slightly worse than 1e-4. This is surprising but suggests 1e-4 is actually near-optimal, possibly because robots occasionally need to recheck.

2. **p0x1 = 0.0015** (machine #4, score 5084.83) — slightly worse than 0.001, confirming 0.001 is better.

3. **LED color 6 (cyan) vs 5 (magenta)** — using cyan LED (cle0=6) gave 4920.23, very close to champion. The LED color shouldn't matter for scoring but may affect inter-robot signaling.

4. **Adding neighbor condition in stop state** (machine #3, score 5054.38) — slightly worse, suggesting the simple 1-transition stop is better.

**The champion parameters seem nearly optimal:**
- p0x0 = 0.999 (green detection)
- p0x1 = 0.001 (random fallback)  
- p1x0 = 1e-4 (very rare restart)

**Strategy:**
- Keep champion exactly
- Try very fine variations around p0x1 (0.0008, 0.0009, 0.0011, 0.0012)
- Try very fine variations around p1x0 (2e-4, 5e-5, 1e-5)
- Try p0x0 = 1.0 explicitly
- Try the 3-state GoAway→GoToGreen→Stop with these optimal params
- Explore: try GoAwayColor with other colors (walls appear as non-green before switch)
- Try adding a second transition in state 0 based on seeing green with lower prob (belt-and-suspenders)

**Avoid:** neighbor conditions in stop state, multiple transitions with color detection in state 0, p1x0=0.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis:**

The champion (4871.02) uses p0x1=0.0013, p1x0=1e-4. The trend is clear:
- p0x1 = 0.0013 > 0.0011 > 0.001 in terms of performance (lower score = better)
- p1x0 = 1e-4 seems optimal
- p0x0 = 0.999 is good (p0x0=1.0 gives slightly worse 5116)

So the random fallback probability p0x1 being around 0.0013 is actually helping — robots that can't see green still eventually stop via this random channel. The sweet spot seems to be slightly above 0.001.

**Exploitation targets:**
- Try p0x1 = 0.0014, 0.0015, 0.0012 (fine-tuning around 0.0013)
- Try p1x0 even lower: 5e-5, 1e-5
- Try p0x1 = 0.0013 with slightly different p1x0 values
- Avoid p0x1 = 0.0008 (5276 score — too low) and p0x1 = 0.0009 (5201 from worst)

**Key insight:** p0x1 around 0.0013 helps robots that are occluded from the green wall still stop. Too low (0.0008) and occluded robots stay moving. Too high (0.002+) and robots stop too early.

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state variant with p0x1=0.0013
- Try GoAwayColor with different movement color (clr0=5 or 4)
- Try adding a second green-detection transition with lower threshold

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of convergence:**

The champion (4729.40) uses p0x1=0.0017, and the trend is clearly showing that higher p0x1 values in the range 0.0013-0.0017 are better:
- 0.0017 → 4729 (champion)
- 0.0016 → 4857
- 0.0014 → 4897
- 0.0013 → 4864-5033 (mixed)
- 0.0012 → 5046
- 0.0011 → 5137

The trend suggests p0x1 should be tried even higher: 0.0018, 0.0019, 0.002, 0.0022, 0.0025.

Also notable:
- p1x0 = 1e-4 remains optimal (2e-4 gives worse results)
- p0x0 = 0.999 remains optimal
- The magenta LED (cle0=5) variant with clr0=5 gave 4864 — very close
- The 3-state variant ties with 2-state at same params

**Strategy for final round:**
1. Champion (exact copy) — must include
2. Exploit: try p0x1 = 0.0018, 0.0019, 0.002, 0.0022, 0.0025, 0.003
3. Try p1x0 = 5e-5 with optimal p0x1
4. Try 3-state variant with p0x1=0.0017
5. Try magenta LED variant with p0x1=0.0017

The p0x1 parameter controls how quickly robots without line-of-sight to the green wall stop. Higher values mean faster stopping for occluded robots, which is good — but too high would cause premature stopping before the switch time.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 19 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.54 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
